\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\frac{\partial\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}|\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\leqslant}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\frac{\partial\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}|^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\leqslant}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{N}}\sup_{\tau\in\mathbf{T}(\theta)}\mathrm{e}^{2\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}b(X_{1})^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{\epsilon}}|\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|^{2}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{<}}\infty,

where the last inequality follows from Assumption
2
(b) and Assumption
1
(g).
∎

Lemma 20
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}=O(T^{-1})
.

Proof.

Unlike in most of the rest of the paper, for clarity, in this proof we do not use the potentially ambiguous notation that denotes
\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}
with
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}
.
14
14

14

This is a potentially ambiguous notation in the sense that
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}
could also denote
\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta}\right|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}_{T}}
. Except when indicated otherwise, such an ambiguity cannot occur because we never use derivatives of
\theta\mapsto L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
(a), by subsection
B.2
(p.
B.2
), the function
L_{T}(\theta,\tau)
is well-defined and twice continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough. Moreover, under Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d)-(h), by Lemma
21
i (p.
21
),
\tau_{T}(.)
is continuously differentiable in
\mathbf{\Theta}
. Now, under Assumption
1
, by Theorem
1
i (p.
1
) and Lemma
2
iii (p.
2
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
\hat{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
and
\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T})\rightarrow\tau(\theta_{0})
, so that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
(\hat{\theta}_{T}^{\prime}\;\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T})^{\prime})
is in any arbitrary small neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})
. Therefore, under Assumption
1
and
2
(a), by the chain rule theorem
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, Chap. 5 sec. 11)
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
\theta\mapsto L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))
is continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\hat{\theta}_{T}
, and, for all
j\in[\![1,m]\!]
,

\displaystyle 0=\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}_{T}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\displaystyle 0=\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}+\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}\left.\frac{\partial\tau(\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}_{T}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}=-\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}\left.\frac{\partial\tau(\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}_{T}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}=O(T^{-1})O(1)=O(T^{-1}).

(a)
It is an immediate and standard implication of the chain rule
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, chap. 5, sec. 12, exercise 3)
.
(b)
Firstly, under Assumptions
1
and
2
, by Lemma
22
iv (p.

),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

because
(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))\rightarrow(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
, by Theorem
1
i (p.

) and Lemma
2
iii (p.
2
). Secondly, under Assumptions

and

, by Theorem

) and Lemma

),
\mathbb{P}

,
\left.\frac{\partial\tau(\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}=O(1)

Lemma 21

(First Derivative of the implicit function
\tau_{T}(.)
)
.

Under Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d)-(h),

(i)

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough, the function
\tau_{T}:\mathbf{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{m}
is continuously differentiable in
\mathbf{\Theta}
and its first derivative is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\tau_{T}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}=\negthickspace-\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\psi_{t}(\theta)\psi_{t}(\theta)^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\negthickspace\left(\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\negthickspace+\negthickspace\psi_{t}(\theta)\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right)\right];

(ii)

for any sequence
(\theta_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{\mathbf{N}}
converging to
\theta_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough, there exists
\bar{\theta}_{T}
between
\theta_{T}
and
\theta_{0}
s.t.
\sqrt{T}[\tau_{T}(\theta_{T})-\tau_{T}(\theta_{0})]=\frac{\partial\tau_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\sqrt{T}(\theta_{T}-\theta_{0})
;

(iii)

under additional Assumptions
1
(c) and
2
(b), for any sequence
(\theta_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{\mathbf{N}}
converging to
\theta_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\frac{\partial\tau_{T}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\rightarrow-\mathbb{E}[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta}\right]
; and

(iv)

under additional Assumptions
1
(c) and
2
(b), for any sequence
(\theta_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{\mathbf{N}}
converging to
\theta_{0}
s.t., as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\sqrt{T}(\theta_{T}-\theta_{0})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\tau_{T}(\theta_{T})-\tau_{T}(\theta_{0})=-V^{-1}M(\theta_{T}-\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(T^{-1/2})
, where

and

.

Proof.

(i)

Under Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d)-(h), by Lemma
1

1
) and its proof,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough, the assumptions of the standard implicit function theorem hold and
\tau_{T}(.)
is continuously differentiable. Thus, under Assumption

(a)(b) and (d)-(h),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough, application of the implicit function theorem yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\tau_{T}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.-\left[\frac{\partial\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\psi_{t}(\theta)\right]}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\left[\frac{\partial\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\psi_{t}(\theta)\right]}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]\right|_{\tau=\tau_{T}(\theta)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.-\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\psi_{t}(\theta)\psi_{t}(\theta)^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\left(\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}+\psi_{t}(\theta)\tau^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right)\right]\right|_{\tau=\tau_{T}(\theta)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\negthickspace-\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\psi_{t}(\theta)\psi_{t}(\theta)^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\negthickspace\left(\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\negthickspace+\negthickspace\psi_{t}(\theta)\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right)\right].

),
\mathbb{P}

\tau_{T}(.)
is continuously differentiable, so that the result follows from a first-order stochastic Taylor-Lagrange expansion
(

.

\displaystyle\ \frac{\partial\tau_{T}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\ \left.+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta_{0})}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta}\right]

because

Remark 3
.

Remark 4
.

In the case in which
\theta_{T}=\hat{\theta}_{T}
, there exist at least one other way to prove Lemma
22
that do not require Assumption
2
. This way follows an approach à la
Newey and Smith 2004
, which relies on ULLN with
\mathbf{T}_{T}(\theta)=\{\tau\in\mathbf{R}^{m}:|\tau|\leqslant T^{-\zeta}\}
and
\zeta>0
. We do not follow this ways because (i) Other parts of the proof of Theorem
1
ii (p.
1
) require the asymptotic normality of
\hat{\theta}_{T}
and thus Assumption
2
; (ii) It would lengthen the proofs and complicate their logic; (iii) We later use Lemma
22
with
\theta_{T}=\check{\theta}_{T}
, where
\check{\theta}_{T}
is a constrained estimator.
\diamond

Lemma 23

(Finiteness of the expectations of supremum of the terms from
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\tau}
and
\frac{\partial^{2}L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\tau}
)
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, there exists a closed ball
\overline{B_{L}}\subset\mathbf{S}
centered at
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
with strictly positive radius s.t., for all
(h,k)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\right]<\infty
;

(ii)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta)|\right]<\infty
;

(iii)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)|\right]<\infty
;

(iv)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}|\right]<\infty
;

(v)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}|\right]<\infty
;

(vi)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta)\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}|\right]<\infty
;

(vii)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|\right]<\infty
;

(viii)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|\right]<\infty
;

(ix)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|\right]<\infty
; and

(x)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\tau^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}|\right]<\infty
.

(x)
The norm of a product of matrices is smaller than the product of the norms
(e.g.,
Rudin 1953
, Theorem 9.7 and note that all norms are equivalent on finite dimensional spaces)
. Thus, for
\overline{B_{L}}
of sufficiently small radius, for all
(\ell,j)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\tau^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}|\right]

\displaystyle(\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\tau|)\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{N}}\sup_{\tau\in\mathbf{T}(\theta)}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}b(X_{1})^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{<}}\infty.

(a)

1
(a)-(e) and (g)-(h), by Lemma

),
\mathbf{S}
contains an open ball centered at
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))

(a)-(e) and (g)-(h), for

\mathbf{S}
,

, because

by Assumption

\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{N}}|\psi(X_{1},\theta)|\leqslant b(X)
and

B.4.
Proof of Theorem
2
: Trinity
+1

The proof of Theorem
2
adapts the traditional way of deriving the trinity along the lines of
Smith 2011
. As in the proof of Theorem
1
, the main difference comes from the complexity of the variance term
|\Sigma_{T}(\theta)|_{\det}
.

Core of the proof of Theorem
2
.

Asymptotic distribution of
\mathrm{Wald}_{T}
.
By Assumption
3
(a),
r:\mathbf{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{q}
is continuously differentiable. Thus, under Assumptions
1
and
2
, if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds, a first-order Taylor-Lagrange expansion at
\theta_{0}
evaluated at
\hat{\theta}_{T}
,
\omega
by
\omega
, yields,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle r(\hat{\theta}_{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(\theta_{0})+R(\bar{\theta}_{T})(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})\text{ , where $\bar{\theta}_{T}$ is between $\hat{\theta}_{T}$ and $\theta_{0}$; }

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle R(\bar{\theta}_{T})(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle\underset{(b)}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D}}{{\rightarrow}}}

\displaystyle R(\theta_{0})\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma(\theta_{0}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(0,R(\theta_{0})\Sigma(\theta_{0})R(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\right).

(a)
By definition, if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds,
r(\theta_{0})=0_{q\times 1}
.
(b)
Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, by Theorem
1
ii (p.
1
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma(\theta_{0}))
, which also implies that
\bar{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
.
Thus, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), by continuity of
R(.)
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
R(\bar{\theta})\rightarrow R(\theta_{0})
, so that the result follows by the Slutsky’s theorem.

Now, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), by Lemma
28
i (p.
28
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
, so that
R(\check{\theta}_{T})\rightarrow R(\theta_{0})
by Assumption
3
(a). Moreover, by the theorem’s assumption, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Sigma(\theta_{0})
. In addition, by Assumption
1
(h) and
3
(b),
\Sigma(\theta_{0})
and
R(\theta_{0})
are full rank, so that
\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}
and
R(\check{\theta}_{T})
are full rank w.p.a.1 as
T\rightarrow\infty
(Lemma
30
p.
30
). Then, the result follows from the Cochran’s theorem.

Asymptotic distribution of
\mathrm{LM}_{T}
.
Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, by Proposition
2
iii (p.
2
), if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.

holds, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\check{\gamma}_{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,(R(\theta_{0})\Sigma(\theta_{0})R(\theta_{0})^{\prime})^{-1})
. Now, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), by Lemma

i (p.

),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
, so that

by Assumption
3
(a).
Moreover, by the theorem’s assumption, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Sigma(\theta_{0})
. In addition, by Assumption
1
(h) and
3
(b),
\Sigma(\theta_{0})
and

are full rank, so that

). Then, by the Cochran’s theorem, as

,
T\check{\gamma}_{T}^{\prime}[R(\check{\theta}_{T})\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}R(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}]\check{\gamma}_{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D}}{{\rightarrow}}\chi_{q}^{2}
.
Finally, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, by
Lemma
28
iii (p.
28
),
R(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}\check{\gamma}_{T}=-\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}
, so
T\check{\gamma}_{T}^{\prime}[R(\check{\theta}_{T})\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}R(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}]\check{\gamma}_{T}=T[R(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}\check{\gamma}_{T}]^{\prime}\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}[R(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}\check{\gamma}_{T}]=\negthickspace T\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}^{\prime}\negthickspace\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace=\frac{\partial\ln[\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\check{\theta}_{T})]}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\widehat{\Sigma(\theta_{0})}\frac{\partial\ln[\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\check{\theta}_{T})]}{\partial\theta}
, where the last equality follows from the definition of the LogESP in Lemma
12
(p.

), i.e.,

L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta)):=\ln\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\right]-\frac{1}{2T}\ln|\Sigma_{T}(\theta)|_{\det}=\frac{1}{T}[\ln(\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\theta))-\frac{m}{2}\ln(\frac{T}{2\pi})].

Asymptotic distribution of
\mathrm{ALR}_{T}
.
Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds, by Lemma

(p.

),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as

,

\displaystyle 2\{\ln[\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\hat{\theta}_{T})]-\ln[\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\check{\theta}_{T})]\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\check{\theta}_{T})\right]^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}\left[\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\check{\theta}_{T})\right]+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\left[\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})\negthickspace+\negthickspace o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\right]^{\prime}\negthickspace\negthickspace\Sigma^{-1}\negthickspace\left[\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})\negthickspace+\negthickspace o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\right]\negthickspace+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{q}

(a)
Use the bilinearity and symmetry of the quadratic form defined by the matrix
\widehat{V}_{T}
, which is symmetric by the theorem’s assumption.
(b)
Firstly, by theorem’s assumption, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

, so that
\widehat{V}_{T}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
. Thus, the second and third terms are
o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

.

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\rightarrow}}
, by the present theorem assumption, as

Lemma 24

(Asymptotic expansions for
\mathrm{ALR}_{T}
)
.

,

and
3

\displaystyle 2\{\ln[\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\hat{\theta}_{T})]-\ln[\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\check{\theta}_{T})]\}=T(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\check{\theta}_{T})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(ii)

\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\check{\theta}_{T})=\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(p.

,

T\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\ M^{\prime}(V^{\prime})^{-1}\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\tau}V^{-1}M-2\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\theta}V^{-1}M

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle M^{\prime}(V^{\prime})^{-1}VV^{-1}M-2M^{\prime}V^{-1}M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-M^{\prime}V^{-1}M=-\Sigma(\theta_{0})^{-1}.

(ii)
Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
, addition and subtraction of
\sqrt{T}\theta_{0}
yield

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\check{\theta}_{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})-\sqrt{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-M^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)-\left[M^{-1}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

where the explanations for the second equality are the following. Firstly, under Assumptions
1
and
2
, by Proposition
1
(p.
1
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\sqrt{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})=-M\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
. Secondly, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and

, by Proposition
2
i (p.
2
), if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.

holds,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

\sqrt{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0})=M^{-1}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.
∎

Lemma 25

(Asymptotic limit of
\frac{\partial^{2}L_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\tau}
)
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, for any sequence
(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}
converging to
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
, for all
(h,k)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

(i)

\frac{\partial^{2}M_{1,T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau_{h}\partial\tau_{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}[\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta_{0})]
;

(ii)

\frac{\partial^{2}M_{2,T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau_{h}\partial\tau_{k}}=O(T^{-1})
;

(iii)

\frac{\partial^{2}M_{3,T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau_{h}\partial\tau_{k}}=O(T^{-1})
; and

(iv)

\frac{\partial^{2}L_{T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau\partial\tau^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

(i)
By equation (
29
) on p.
29
, for all
(h,k)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}M_{1,T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau_{h}\partial\tau_{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\negthickspace\left(1-\frac{m}{2T}\right)\frac{1}{\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{i}(\theta_{T})}\right]^{2}}\left\{\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{i}(\theta_{T})}\right]\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,h}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t,k}(\theta_{T})\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.\negthickspace-\negthickspace\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,h}(\theta_{T})\right]\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{i}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{i,k}(\theta_{T})\right]\negthickspace\right\}.

where, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
(\theta_{T}\hskip 9.24994pt\tau_{T})\rightarrow(\theta_{0}\hskip 9.24994pt\tau(\theta_{0}))
by assumption. Now, under Assumptions
1
and

, by Lemma

i-iii (p.

), for
\overline{B_{L}}
a ball around

of sufficiently small radius,
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\right]<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta)|\right]<\infty
, and

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\overline{B_{L}}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1,}\theta)}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta)|\right]
. Thus, by Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d), the ULLN (uniform law of large numbers) à la Wald
(e.g.,

, pp. 24-25, Theorem 1.3.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}M_{1,T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau_{k}\partial\tau_{h}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}]^{2}}\left\{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}]\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta_{0})]\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta_{0})]\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta_{0})]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\psi_{k}(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi_{h}(X_{1},\theta_{0})]

because

by Assumption

by Lemma

) under Assumption

(a)-(e) and (g)-(h).

1
, by equation (

) on p.

,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for

big enough, for all

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}M_{2,T}(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})}{\partial\tau_{h}\partial\tau_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{T}{\rm tr}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,k}(\theta_{T})\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\times\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\left.\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,h}(\theta_{T})\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}{\rm tr}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,k}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t,h}(\theta_{T})\frac{\partial\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]\right\}.\

where, as

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\left.\times\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,h}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})^{\prime}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2T}{\rm tr}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})}\psi_{t,k}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t,h}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})\psi_{t}(\theta_{T})^{\prime}\right]\right\}

Proposition 2

(Asymptotic normality of
\check{\theta}_{T}
,
\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})
and
\check{\gamma}_{T}
)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

(i)

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\begin{bmatrix}\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0}\\
\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})\\
\check{\gamma}_{T}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\hskip 9.24994ptM^{-1}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
; and

(ii)

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\begin{bmatrix}\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0}\\
\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})\\
\check{\gamma}_{T}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\hskip 9.24994pt\Sigma^{1/2^{\prime}}P_{\Sigma^{1/2}R^{\prime}}^{\perp}\Sigma^{-1/2^{\prime}}M^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}\Sigma^{-1/2}P_{\Sigma^{1/2}R^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-1/2^{\prime}}M^{-1}&\\
-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\psi_{t}(\theta_{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(iii)

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\begin{bmatrix}\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0}\\
\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})\\
\check{\gamma}_{T}\\
\end{bmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\begin{bmatrix}(\Sigma^{1/2})^{\prime}P^{\perp}_{\Sigma^{1/2}R^{\prime}}\Sigma^{1/2}&0_{m\times m}&0_{m\times q}\\
0_{m\times m}&(V^{1/2})^{-1}P_{\Sigma^{1/2}R^{\prime}}(V^{1/2^{\prime}})^{-1}&-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
0_{q\times m}&-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\right)
,

where
\Sigma:=\Sigma(\theta_{0}):=M^{-1}V(M^{\prime})^{-1}
,
M:=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]
,
V:=\mathbb{E}[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}]
, and
R:=\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

(i)-(ii)
The function
L_{T}(\theta,\tau)
is well-defined and twice continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough by subsection
B.2
(p.
B.2
), under Assumptions
1
and
2
(a). Similarly, the function
S_{T}(\theta,\tau):=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\psi_{t}(\theta)
and
\theta\mapsto r(\theta)
are continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})
by Assumption
1
(a)(b) and
3
(a). Now, under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
(a), by Lemma
28
i (p.
28
), Lemma
2
iii (p.
2
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
and
\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})\rightarrow\tau(\theta_{0})
, so that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
(\check{\theta}_{T}^{\prime}\;\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime})
is in any arbitrary small neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})
. Therefore, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), stochastic first-order Taylor-Lagrange expansions
(
Jennrich 1969
, Lemma 3)
around
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
evaluated at
(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))
yield,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{L}_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{L}_{T}(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))}{\partial\theta}+\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}\partial\theta}\left(\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0}\right)+\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\theta}\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})

\displaystyle S_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{T}(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))+\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\left(\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0}\right)+\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})

\displaystyle r(\check{\theta}_{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(\theta_{0})+\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\left(\check{\theta}_{T}-\theta_{0}\right)

because
\tau(\theta_{0})=0_{m\times 1}
by Lemma
10
iv (p.
10
), and where

because

Denote the final asymptotic variance matrix with

Lemma 26
.

Using the notation of Proposition
2
(p.
2
), under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
,

(i)

for any sequence
(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}
converging to
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\\
\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}&0\\
\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}0_{m\times m}&\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]^{\prime}&\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]&\mathbb{E}\left[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}\right]&0\\
\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}

;

(ii)

\begin{bmatrix}0_{m\times m}&\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]^{\prime}&\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]&\mathbb{E}\left[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}\right]&0\\
\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}
is invertible, so that, for any sequence
(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}
converging to
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for
T
big enough, the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\\
\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}&0\\
\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}
is invertible; and

(iii)

for any sequence
(\theta_{T},\tau_{T})_{T\in\mathbf{N}}
converging to
(\theta_{0},\tau(\theta_{0}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial^{2}{L}_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}\partial\theta}&\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\\
\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial S_{T}(\bar{\theta}_{T},\bar{\tau}_{T})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}&0\\
\frac{\partial r(\bar{\theta}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\\
\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma+\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994ptM^{-1}\negthickspace-\negthickspace\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}\negthickspace-\negthickspace(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&\;-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}
.

Proof.

(i)
Under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), it follows from the continuity of
\frac{\partial r(.)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}
, which is implied by Assumption
3
(a), and Lemma
14
ii and iii (p.
14
) and Lemma
17
(p.
17
), given that
\tau(\theta_{0})=0_{m\times 1}
by Lemma
10
ii (p.
10
) and Assumption
1
(c), under Assumption
1
(a)(b)(d)(e)(g) and (h).

(ii)
It is sufficient to check the assumptions of Corollary
2
i (p.
2
) with
A=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c }0_{m\times m}&M^{\prime}\\
M&\ V\end{array}\right]
and
B=\begin{bmatrix}R^{\prime}\\
0_{m\times q}\\
\end{bmatrix}
in order to establish the first part of the statement. Firstly, under Assumptions
1
and
2
, by Lemma
13
iii (p.
13
),
A=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c }0_{m\times m}&M^{\prime}\\
M&\ V\end{array}\right]
is invertible. Secondly, by Assumptions
1
(h) and
3
(b),
(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)=-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})
is also invertible. Then, the second
part of the statement follows from a trivial case of the Lemma
30
(p.
30
).

(iii)
Under Assumption
1
(a)(b)(c)(d)(e)(g)(h), by the statement (ii) of the present lemma, the limiting matrix is invertible. Thus, using the notation of Proposition
2
(p.
2
),

\displaystyle\ \begin{bmatrix}0_{m\times m}&\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]^{\prime}&\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]&\mathbb{E}\left[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}\right]&0\\
\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0_{m\times m}&M^{\prime}&R^{\prime}\\
M&V&0_{m\times q}\\
R&0_{q\times m}&0_{q\times q}\\
\end{bmatrix}^{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma+\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994ptM^{-1}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&\hskip 9.24994pt-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}&(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}

where the explanation for the last equality is as follows. Apply Corollary
2
ii (p.
2
) with
A=\begin{bmatrix}0_{m\times m}&M^{\prime}\\
M&V\end{bmatrix}
and
B=\begin{bmatrix}R^{\prime}\\
0_{m\times q}\\
\end{bmatrix}
, and note that, by Lemma
27
iii, iv and vi (p.
27
),

\displaystyle A^{-1}-A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)B^{\prime}A^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma+\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994ptM^{-1}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994pt(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}.

Then, the result follows from the continuity of the inverse transformation
(e.g.,
Rudin 1953
, Theorem 9.8)
.
∎

Lemma 27
.

Let
A=\begin{bmatrix}0_{m\times m}&M^{\prime}\\
M&V\end{bmatrix}
and
B=\begin{bmatrix}R^{\prime}\\
0_{m\times q}\\
\end{bmatrix}
where

\Sigma:=\Sigma(\theta_{0}):=M^{-1}V(M^{\prime})^{-1}
,
M:=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]
,
V:=\mathbb{E}[\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})^{\prime}]
, and
R=\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}
. Then, under Assumption
1
(a)(b)(h) and
3
(b), the following equalities hold

(i)

\displaystyle A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma&M^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}&0_{m\times m}\\
\end{bmatrix}
;

(ii)

\displaystyle A^{-1}B=\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma R^{\prime}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}\\
\end{bmatrix}
, so that
B^{\prime}A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994ptRM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}
;

(iii)

\displaystyle(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)^{-1}=-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}
;

(iv)

\displaystyle A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}
;

(v)

\displaystyle A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)B^{\prime}A^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 18.49988pt\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994pt-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}
; and

(vi)

\displaystyle A^{-1}-A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)B^{\prime}A^{-1}\\
=\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma+\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994ptM^{-1}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994pt(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}
.

Proof.

(i)
It corresponds to a part of Lemma
13
iii (p.
13
) under Assumptions
1
and
2
.

(ii)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma&M^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}&\hskip 18.49988pt0_{m\times m}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}R^{\prime}\\
0_{m\times q}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma R^{\prime}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}B

(iii)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}R&0_{q\times m}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma R^{\prime}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-R\Sigma R^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=B^{\prime}A^{-1}B

(iv)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma R^{\prime}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)^{-1}

(v)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-R\Sigma&\hskip 18.49988ptRM^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 18.49988pt\Sigma R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\\
(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}R\Sigma&\hskip 9.24994pt-(M^{\prime})^{-1}R^{\prime}(R\Sigma R^{\prime})^{-1}RM^{-1}\end{bmatrix}=A^{-1}B(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B)B^{\prime}A^{-1}

∎

Lemma 28

(Constrained estimator and its Lagrangian)
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2

and
3
(a), if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,

(i)

the constrained estimator
\check{\theta}_{T}
exists, and
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
, as
T\rightarrow\infty
;

(ii)

\theta\mapsto L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))
is continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\check{\theta}_{T}
;

(iii)

under additional Assumption
3
(b), there exists a unique vector,
\check{\gamma}_{T}
, called the Lagrangian multiplier, s.t.
\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}+\frac{\partial r(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\check{\gamma}_{T}=0_{m\times 1}
;

(iv)

under additional Assumption
3
(b),
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}+\frac{\partial r(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime}}{\partial\theta}\check{\gamma}_{T}=O(T^{-1})
, as
T\rightarrow\infty
, where
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}:=\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}
.

Proof.

(i)
The constrained set
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}:=\{\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}:r(\theta)=0\}
is bounded as a subset of the compact (and thus bounded) set
\mathbf{\Theta}
. The constrained set
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}
is also closed: For all
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\in\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{\mathbf{N}}
s.t.
\lim_{n\rightarrow}\theta_{n}=\bar{\theta}
,
\bar{\theta}\in\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}
because (i) by compactness of
\mathbf{\Theta}
,
\bar{\theta}\in\mathbf{\Theta}
; and (ii) by the continuity of
r:\mathbf{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{q}
(i.e., Assumption
3
(a)),
r(\bar{\theta})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}r(\theta_{n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}0=0
. Therefore, the constrained set
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}
is itself compact. Moreover, under Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d)-(h), by Lemma
1
ii-iii (p.
1
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
\theta\mapsto\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\theta)
is continuous and, for all
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}
,
\omega\mapsto\hat{f}_{\theta^{*}_{T}}(\theta)
is measurable. Thus, the existence and the measurability of the constrained estimator
\check{\theta}_{T}
follows from the Schmetterer-Jennrich lemma (
Schmetterer 1966
Chap. 5 Lemma 3.3;
Jennrich 1969
Lemma 2).

In order to establish the consistency of
\check{\theta}_{T}
, it remains to check the other assumptions of the standard consistency theorem
(e.g.
Newey and McFadden 1994
, pp. 2121-2122 Theorem 2.1, which is also valid in an almost-sure sense)
, where the constrained set
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}:=\{\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}:r(\theta)=0\}
is the parameter space. Because
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\subset\mathbf{\Theta}
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}}\left|\ln\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\right]-\frac{1}{2T}\ln|\Sigma_{T}(\theta)|_{\det}-\ln\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}}\left|\ln\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{T}(\theta)^{\prime}\psi_{t}(\theta)}\right]-\frac{1}{2T}\ln|\Sigma_{T}(\theta)|_{\det}-\ln\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]\right|\rightarrow 0

where the convergence to zero follows from equation (
18
) on p.
18
, under Assumption
1
. In addition, under Assumption
1
(a)-(e) and (g)-(h), by Lemma
10
iv (p.
10
),
\theta\mapsto\ln\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]
is uniquely maximized at
\theta_{0}
, i.e., for all
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}\setminus\{\theta_{0}\}
,
\ln\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]<\ln\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta_{0})}]=0
, and, under Assumptions
1
(a)(b)(d)(e)(g) and (h), by Lemma
3
(p.
3
),
\theta\mapsto\ln\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]
is continuous in
\tilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\subset\mathbf{\Theta}
.

(ii)
Under Assumptions
1
and
2
(a), by subsection
B.2
(p.
B.2
), the function
L_{T}(\theta,\tau)
is well-defined and twice continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough. Moreover, under Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d)-(h), by Lemma
21
i (p.
21
),
\tau_{T}(.)
is continuously differentiable in
\mathbf{\Theta}
. Now, under Assumption
1
, by the statement (i) of the present lemma and Lemma
2
iii (p.
2
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
and
\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})\rightarrow\tau(\theta_{0})
, so that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
(\check{\theta}_{T}^{\prime}\;\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T})^{\prime})
is in any arbitrary small neighborhood of
(\theta_{0}^{\prime}\;\tau(\theta_{0})^{\prime})
. Therefore, under Assumption
1
and
2
(a), by the chain rule theorem
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, Chap. 5 sec. 11)
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
\theta\mapsto L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))
is continuously differentiable at
\check{\theta}_{T}
.

(iii)
It is a consequence of the Lagrange theorem
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, Chap. 7 sec. 12)
. Check its assumptions. Firstly, under Assumptions
1
and
2
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. by the statement (i) of the present lemma,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough, the constrained estimator
\check{\theta}_{T}
exists and that it is in the interior of
\mathbf{\Theta}
by consistency and Assumption
1
(c). Then, we should check the other assumptions of the Lagrange theorem
\omega
by
\omega
on the subset of
\mathbf{\Omega}
where
\check{\theta}_{T}
exists.
Firstly, by Assumption
3
(a),
r:\mathbf{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{q}
is continuously differentiable. Secondly, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), if the test hypothesis (
9
) on p.
9
holds,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
, and, by Assumption
3
(b),
\frac{\partial r(\theta_{0})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}
is full rank, Thus,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
\frac{\partial r(\check{\theta}_{T})}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}
is full rank
by continuity of the determinant function. Finally, by the statement (iv) of the present lemma
\theta\mapsto L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))
is differentiable at
\check{\theta}_{T}
.

(iv)
First of all, note that it does
not
immediately follow from the statement (iii) because
\frac{\partial L_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\hat{\theta}_{T}))}{\partial\theta}
denotes
\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}
instead of
\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta}\right|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}_{T}}
(see footnote
14
on p.
14
). Under Assumption
1
(a)(b) and (d)-(h), by Lemma
21
i (p.
21
),
\tau_{T}(.)
is continuously differentiable in
\mathbf{\Theta}
. Moreover, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), if by the statement (ii) of the present lemma,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough,
\theta\mapsto L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))
is continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\check{\theta}_{T}
.
Thus,
by an immediate and standard implication of the chain rule
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, chap. 5, sec. 12, exercise 3)
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for
T
big enough, for all
j\in[\![1,m]\!]
,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau_{T}(\theta))}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}+\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}\left.\frac{\partial\tau(\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}

(68)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}+O(T^{-1})

where the explanations for the last equality are as follow.
Firstly, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), by Lemma
22
iv (p.
22
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\left.\frac{\partial L_{T}(\theta,\tau)^{\prime}}{\partial\tau}\right|_{(\theta,\tau)=(\check{\theta}_{T},\tau_{T}(\check{\theta}_{T}))}=O(T^{-1})
because
\check{\theta}_{T}\rightarrow\theta_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
, by the second part of the statement (i) of the present lemma. Secondly, under Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
(a), by the second part of the statement (i) of the present lemma and Lemma
21
iii (p.
21
),
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\left.\frac{\partial\tau(\theta)}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right|_{\theta=\check{\theta}_{T}}=O(1)
.

Now the results follows by plugging the above equation (
68
) into the Lagrangian FOC of the statement (iii) of the present lemma.
∎

Appendix C
On the assumptions

C.1.
Discussion

Assumptions
1
and
2
are mainly adapted from the entropy literature. Assumption
1
(a) ensures the basic requirement for inference, that is, data contain different pieces of information (independence) about the same phenomenon (identically distributed). The conditions “independence and identically distributed” are much stronger than needed, and can be relaxed to allow for time dependence along the lines of
Kitamura and Stutzer 1997
.
We restrain ourself to the i.i.d. case for brevity and clarity.
Assumption
1
(a) also requires completeness of the probability space so that we can define functions only a probability-one subset of
\mathbf{\Omega}
without generating potential measurability complications. The completeness of the probability space is without significant loss of generality
(e.g.,
Kallenberg 2002 (1997
, p. 13)
, and it is often implicitly or explicitly required in the literature.

Assumption
1
(b) mainly requires standard regularity conditions for the moment function
\psi(.,.)
. As usual in nonlinear econometrics, the existence of the estimator relies on such regularity conditions. An alternative would be to rely on empirical process theory, but it seems here inappropriate as the implicit nature of the definition of the ESP approximation requires smooth functions.
We require Assumption
1
(b), as well as some of the following assumptions, to hold in an
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the parameter space
\mathbf{\Theta}
, so that we can deal with its boundary
\partial\mathbf{\Theta}
in the same way as with its interior. In particular, it ensures that
\Sigma(\theta)
is invertible for
\theta\in\partial\mathbf{\Theta}
under probability measures equivalent to
\mathbb{P}
(Corollary
1
ii on p.
1
), and it allows to apply an implicit function theorem to
\tau(\theta)
, also for
\theta\in\partial\mathbf{\Theta}
(Lemma
10
on p.
10
). For the latter reason, the entropy literature often appears to also (implicitly) assume that assumptions hold in an
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the parameter space.
In applications, this is often innocuous as the boundary of the parameter space is often loosely specified. However, in some specific situations, which we rule out, this may be problematic
(e.g.,
Andrews 1999
, and references therein)
.

Assumption
1
(c)
requires global identification, which is a necessary condition to prove the consistency of an estimator. If we were interested in the ESP approximation instead of its maximizer (i.e., the ESP estimator), global identification could be relaxed as
Holcblat 2012
and a companion paper show. Assumption
1
(c)
also requires equality between the dimension of the parameter space and the number of moment conditions, i.e., just-identified moment conditions. We impose the latter for mainly three reasons. Firstly, it appears reasonable to investigate the ESP estimator in the just-identified case before moving to the over-identified case, which requires to generalize the ESP approximation. Secondly, the just-identified case makes clear the difference between the ESP estimator and the existing alternatives, which are all equal in this case (see section
2.2
). Thirdly, this is a standard assumption in the saddlepoint literature. However, note that (i) this assumption is less restrictive than it seems at first sight because, in the linear case, over-identified moment conditions correspond to just-identified moment conditions through the FOCs, and, in the nonlinear case, we can transform over-identified estimating equations into just-identified estimating equations through an extension of
the parameter space
(e.g.,
Newey and McFadden 1994
, p. 2232)
; (ii) ongoing work show how to generalize the ESP approximation to over-identified moment conditions.

Assumption
1
(d) requires the compactness of the parameter space
\mathbf{\Theta}
, and the existence of a solution
\tau(\theta)\in\mathbf{R}^{m}
that solves the equation
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\right]=0
, for all
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}
.
Schennach 2005
also makes this assumption. Compactness of the parameter space is a convenient standard mathematical assumption that is
often relevant in practice. A computer can only handle a bounded parameter space —finite memory of a computer. Regarding the existence of
\tau(\theta)
, it is necessary to ensure the asymptotic existence of the ESP approximation. From a theoretical point of view, the existence of
\tau(\theta)
looks like a reasonable assumption : If, for some
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}
,
0_{m\times 1}
is outside the convex hull of the support of
\psi(X_{1},\theta)
, there is not such a solution
\tau(\theta)
, which also means that
\theta
cannot be
\theta_{0}
, so that it should be excluded from the parameter space. However, the existence of
\tau(\theta)
might be difficult to check in practice. A way to get around this assumption is to (i) assume the existence of
\tau(\theta)
only in a neighbohood of
\theta_{0}
; and (ii) to set the ESP approximation to zero for the
\theta
values that do not have a solution to the finite-sample moment conditions (
15
).
Holcblat 2012
follows such an approach. We do not follow such an approach because it significantly complicates the proofs and the presentation.

Assumptions
1
(e) and
2
(b) rule out fat-tailed distributions. More precisely, they require the existence of exponential moments. They are necessary to apply the the ULLN (uniform law of large numbers) à la Wald
(e.g.,
Ghosh and Ramamoorthi 2003
, pp. 24-25, Theorem 1.3.3)
to components of the ESP approximation. Assumptions
1
(e) and
2
(b) are stronger than the
moment existence assumption in
Hansen 1982
, but they are a common type of assumptions in the
entropy literature
(
Haberman 1984
,
Kitamura and Stutzer 1997
,
Schennach 2007
, e.g.,)
, the saddlepoint literature
(
Almudevar et al. 2000
, e.g.,)
and the literature on exponential models
(
Berk 1972
, e.g.,)
. In particular, Assumptions
1
(d) and
2
(b) are a convenient variant of Assumptions 3.4 and 3.5 in
Schennach 2007
. Both in
Schennach 2007
and in the present paper, the successful estimation of the Hall and Horowitz model, which does not satisfy Assumptions
1
(e) and
2
(b), suggests that the latter can be relaxed. In practice, Assumptions
1
(e) and
2
(b) are not as strong as it may appear because observable quantities have finite support (finite memory of computers), which, in turn, implies that they have all finite moments. Moreover, in the case in which unboundedness is a concern (e.g., moment conditions derived from a likelihood),
Ronchetti and Trojani 2001
provide a way to bound moment functions.

Assumptions
1
(f) and (g) play the same role as Assumptions
1
(e) and
2
(b), although they are less stringent. Assumption
1
(h) requires the invertibility of the asymptotic variance
of standard estimators (scaled by
\sqrt{T}
)
of any solution to the tilted moment condition. In the present paper, this assumption has two main roles. Firstly, it ensures that the determinant term
\left|\Sigma_{T}(\theta)\right|_{\det}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in the ESP approximation (
12
) does not explode, asymptotically.
Secondly, it ensures the positive definiteness of the symmetric matrix
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}\right]
for all
(\theta,\tau)\in\mathbf{S}
, so that the
\min_{\tau\in\mathbf{R}^{m}}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]
is a strictly convex problem, which, in turn, implies the unicity of its solution
\tau(\theta)
. In the setup of the present paper, Assumption
1
(g) is equivalent to the invertibility of
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\frac{\partial\psi(X_{1},\theta)}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}\right]
, for all
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}
(Lemma
29
on p.
29
with
\mathrm{P}=\mathbb{P}
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}}
). In this way, it is stronger than the Assumption 4 in
Kitamura and Stutzer 1997
, but it is close to
Stock and Wright 2000
. Note that
Schennach 2007
also implicitly assumes that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}\right]
is full rank for all
(\theta,\tau)\in\mathbf{S}
, because
Schennach 2007
regards
\tau(\theta)
as a solution to a strictly convex problem
(e.g.,
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal 1993/1996
, chap. 4, Theorem 4.3.1)
. Assumption
1
(g) should often be reasonable because the set of singular matrices has zero Lebesgue measure in the space of square matrices.
15
15

15

The set of singular matrices corresponds to the set of zeros of the determinant, which is nonzero polynomial in several variables. Moreover, by induction over the number of variables with the fundamental theorem of algebra for the base step, a nonzero polynomials has a finite number of zeros.

C.2.
Implications of Assumption
1
(h)

Lemma 29
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Omega}_{A},\mathcal{A})
be a measurable space,
Z:\mathbf{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{k}
be a
k
-dimensional random vectors with
k\in[\![1,\infty[\![
and
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
two probability measures on
(\mathbf{\Omega}_{A},\mathcal{A})
. Denote the expectation and the variance under
\mathrm{P}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}
and
\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{P}}
, respectively.

(i)

For all
\tau\in\mathbf{R}^{k}
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}Z}ZZ^{\prime}\right)\geqslant 0
, it is a positive semi-definite symmetric matrix.

(ii)

If
\mathrm{P}\sim\mathrm{Q}
(i.e., they are equivalent),
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}(|ZZ^{\prime}|)<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}(|ZZ^{\prime}|)<\infty
, then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}(ZZ^{\prime})
invertible
\Leftrightarrow

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}(ZZ^{\prime})
invertible

Proof.

(i)
Symmetry follows from the invariance under transposition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}\left(ZZ^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}Z}\right)
. It remains to show positive semi-definiteness.
For all
y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}
,

\displaystyle\forall\omega\in\mathbf{\Omega},\hskip 9.24994pty^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}Z}ZZ^{\prime}y=\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}Z}[y^{\prime}Z]^{2}\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle y^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}Z}ZZ^{\prime}\right]y=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}\left[y^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}Z}ZZ^{\prime}y\right]\geqslant 0.

where the implication follows from the monotonicity of the Lebesgue integral
(e.g.,
Monfort 1997
, p. 47)
.

(ii)
By contraposition, it is equivalent to prove that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}(ZZ^{\prime})
noninvertible iff
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}(ZZ^{\prime})
noninvertible. By statement (i),

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}(ZZ^{\prime})
noninvertible

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\exists y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0_{k\times 1}\}:

y^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}(ZZ^{\prime})y=0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\exists y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0_{k\times 1}\}:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}[(y^{\prime}Z)^{2}]=0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\exists y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0_{k\times 1}\}:(y^{\prime}Z)^{2}=0\text{ $\mathrm{P}$-a.s.}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\exists y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0_{k\times 1}\}:(y^{\prime}Z)^{2}=0\text{ $\mathrm{Q}$-a.s.}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\exists y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0_{k\times 1}\}:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}[(y^{\prime}Z)^{2}]=0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}

\exists y\in\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0_{k\times 1}\}:

y^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}(ZZ^{\prime})y=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}(ZZ^{\prime})
noninvertible

(a)

y^{\prime}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}(ZZ^{\prime})y=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}[y^{\prime}Z(y^{\prime}Z)^{\prime}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}}[(y^{\prime}Z)^{2}]

(b)
The integral of a positive function w.r.t a measure is null iff the function is null almost-surely
(e.g.,
Kallenberg 2002 (1997
, Lemma 1.24)
.
(c)
By assumption,
\mathrm{P}\sim\mathrm{Q}
.
(d)
Same as (b).
(a)
Same as (a) with
\mathrm{Q}
instead of
\mathrm{P}
.

∎

Corollary 1

(Implication of Assumption
1
(h))
.

Under Assumptions
1
(a)-(b), (e) and (g), Assumption
1
(h) implies that, for all
(\theta,\tau)\in\mathbf{S}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}\right]
is a positive definite symmetric matrix.

Proof.

By Lemma
29
i (p.
29
) with
Z=\psi(X_{1},\theta)
, it is a positive semi-definite matrix. Thus, it remains to show that it is invertible, i.e., definite instead of only semi-definite.

Under Assumption
1
(a)(b)(d)(e)(g) and (h), by Lemma
3
(p.
3
) and Assumption
1
(d)(e), for all
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}
,
0<\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]<\infty
. Moreover, by Assumption
1
(h), for all
\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}\right]
is invertible, so that
\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}\right]
is also invertible. For every
(\theta,\tau)\in\mathbf{S}
, check the assumptions of Lemma
29
ii (p.
29
) with
Z=\psi(X_{1},\theta)
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]}
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}_{(\theta,\tau)}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{\theta}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]\mathrm{e}^{\tau(\theta)^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}}
, so that
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}_{(\theta,\tau)}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]}
. Firstly, for all
(\omega,\tau,\theta)\in\mathbf{\Omega}\times\mathbf{T}\times\mathbf{\Theta}
,
0<\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}_{(\theta,\tau)}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{\theta}}
and
0<\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}
, so that
\mathrm{Q}_{(\theta,\tau)}\sim\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\sim\mathbb{P}
. Secondly, by monotonicity of integration and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, for all
\dot{\theta}\in\mathbf{\Theta}
,
\mathbb{E}[|\psi(X_{1},\dot{\theta})\psi(X_{1},\dot{\theta})^{\prime}|]\leqslant\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}}|\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|]<\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\theta\in\mathbf{\Theta}}|\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|^{2}]}<\infty
, where the last inequality follows from Assumption
1
(g). Thirdly, under Assumption
1
(a)(b)(d)(e)(g) awnd (h), by Lemma
3
(p.
3
) and Assumption
1
(d)(e), for all
(\theta,\tau)\in\mathbf{S}
,
0<\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}]<\infty
. Moreover, under Assumptions
1
(a)-(b), (e) and (g),
by Lemma
8
i (p.
8
),
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{(\theta,\tau)\in\mathbf{S}}|\mathrm{e}^{\tau^{\prime}\psi(X_{1},\theta)}\psi(X_{1},\theta)\psi(X_{1},\theta)^{\prime}|\right]<\infty
, so that, for all

.
Thus, for each

, apply Lemma

) to show the result.

Appendix D
Remaining technical results

Lemma 30

(Asymptotic invertibility of sequence of matrix functions)
.

Let
A(\gamma)
be a family of invertible matrices indexed by
\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
s.t.
\gamma\mapsto A(\gamma)
is continuous, and where
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a compact subset of a Euclidean space. Let
(A_{T}(\gamma))_{T\in[\![1,\infty[\![}
be a sequence of square matrices. If, as
T\rightarrow\infty
,
\sup_{\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}}|A_{T}(\gamma)-A(\gamma)|\rightarrow 0
, then there exist a constant
\varepsilon_{A}>0
and
T_{A}\in\mathbf{N}
s.t. for all
T\in[\![T_{A},\infty[\![
, for all
\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
,
\left||A_{T}(\gamma)|_{\det}\right|\geqslant\varepsilon_{A}
.

Proof.

The function
A\mapsto||A|_{\det}|
is a continuous function. Moreover, by assumption, for all
\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
,
||A(\gamma)|_{\det}|>0
. Thus, by continuity of
\gamma\mapsto A(\gamma)
and compactness of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{A}
s.t.
\min_{\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}}\left||A(\gamma)|_{\det}\right|>2\varepsilon_{A}
. Now continuity of
A\mapsto||A|_{\det}|
on the compact set
\mathbf{\Gamma}
implies uniform continuity
(e.g.,
Rudin 1953
, Theorem 4.19)
, so that there exists
T_{\varepsilon_{A}}\in\mathbf{N}
s.t., for all
T\in[\![T_{\varepsilon_{A}},\infty[\![
,
\sup_{\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}}\left|||A_{T}(\gamma)|_{\det}|-||A(\gamma)|_{\det}|\right|\leqslant\varepsilon_{A}
. Then, for all
\gamma\in\mathbf{\Gamma}
, the triangle inequality
||A(\gamma)|_{\det}|\leqslant\left|||A(\gamma)|_{\det}|-||A_{T}(\gamma)|_{\det}|\right|+||A_{T}(\gamma)|_{\det}|
implies that
\varepsilon_{A}=2\varepsilon_{A}-\varepsilon_{A}\leqslant||A(\gamma)|_{\det}|-\left|||A(\gamma)|_{\det}|-||A_{T}(\gamma)|_{\det}|\right|\leqslant||A_{T}(\gamma)|_{\det}|
.
∎

Lemma 31

(Asymptotic positivity and definiteness of matrices)
.

Let
(A_{T})_{T\geqslant 1}
a sequence of square matrices converging to
A
as
T\rightarrow\infty
.
16
16

16

Note that we do not need to specify the norm as all norms are equivalent in finite-dimensional spaces.
Then, if
(A_{T})_{T\geqslant 1}
is a sequence of symmetric matrices and
A
is a positive-definite matrix (p-d.m), then there exists
\dot{T}\in\mathbf{N}
such that
T\geqslant\dot{T}
implies
A_{T}
is p-d.m.

Proof.

On one hand,
A_{T}
is a p-d.m. if and only if all its eigenvalues are strictly positive
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, Ch. 1 Sec. 13 Theorem 8)
. On the other hand,
\min\mathrm{sp}A_{T}=\min_{z:\|z\|=1}z^{\prime}A_{T}z
, where
\mathrm{sp}A_{T}
denotes the set of eigenvalues of
A

(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, Ch. 11 Sec. 5)
. Thus, it is sufficient to prove that
\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\min_{z:\|z\|=1}z^{\prime}A_{T}z=\min_{z:\|z\|=1}z^{\prime}Az
, which in turn implies that it is sufficient to prove that
\sup_{z:\|z\|=1}|z^{\prime}A_{T}z-z^{\prime}Az|\rightarrow 0
, as
T\rightarrow\infty
. Prove this last result by contradiction.

Assume that
\sup_{z:\|z\|=1}|z^{\prime}A_{T}z-z^{\prime}Az|
does not converge to
0
as
T\rightarrow\infty
. Then, there exists
\varepsilon>0
and an increasing function
\alpha_{1}:\mathbf{N}\mapsto\mathbf{N}
defining a subsequence of vectors of norm
1
,
\left(z_{\alpha_{1}(T)}\right)_{T\geqslant 1}
, and a subsequence of matrices,
\left(A_{\alpha_{1}(T)}\right)_{T\geqslant 1}
, such that

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\left|z_{\alpha_{1}(T)}^{\prime}A_{\alpha_{1}(T)}z_{\alpha_{1}(T)}-z_{\alpha_{1}(T)}^{\prime}Az_{\alpha_{1}(T)}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|z_{\alpha_{1}(T)}^{\prime}\left(A_{\alpha_{1}(T)}-A\right)z_{\alpha_{1}(T)}\right|\leqslant\sum_{(k,l)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}}\left|\left[a_{\alpha_{1}(T)}^{(k,l)}-a^{(k,l)}\right]z^{(k)}_{\alpha_{1}(T)}z^{(l)}_{\alpha_{1}(T)}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\leqslant m^{2}\times\max_{(k,l)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}}\left|a_{\alpha_{1}(T)}^{(k,l)}-a^{(k,l)}\right|

where
m
is the size of the matrix
A
and
a^{(k,l)}
denotes the component of the matrix
A
in the
k
th row and
l
th column. Now, by assumption, using the max norm,
\max_{(k,l)\in[\![1,m]\!]^{2}}\left|a_{\alpha_{1}(T)}^{(k,l)}-a^{(k,l)}\right|\rightarrow 0
as
T\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, there is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 32

(Differential of a log of a squared determinant)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be an open set of
\mathbf{R}^{q}
with
q\in[\![1,\infty[\![
, and
F:\mathbf{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}^{m\times m}
a differentiable function on
\mathbf{G}
. Then
|F|_{\det}:\mathbf{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
is also differentiable on
\mathbf{G}
. Moreover, if
|F(x)|_{\det}\neq 0
where
x\in\mathbf{G}
, then

(i)

D|F(x)|_{\det}=|F(x)|_{\det}{\rm tr}[F(x)^{-1}DF(x)]
;

(ii)

D\ln[|F(x)|_{\det}^{2}]=2{\rm tr}[F(x)^{-1}DF(x)]
.

Proof.

(i)
It is a consequence of the so-called Jacobi’s formula
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, chap. 8 sec. 3)
.

(ii)
First of all, note that the logarithm is well-defined as its argument is strictly positive by assumption. Then, by the statement (i) of the present lemma and the chain rule,

\displaystyle D\ln[|F(x)|_{\det}^{2}]=\frac{1}{|F(x)|_{\det}^{2}}2|F(x)|_{\det}|F(x)|_{\det}{\rm tr}[F(x)^{-1}DF(x)].

∎

Lemma 33

(Inverse of a
2\times 2
partitioned matrix)
.

Let
F
be a square matrix s.t.

\displaystyle F=\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\\
\end{bmatrix}

where
A
and
D
are square matrices. Then, the following statements hold.

(i)

If
A
is invertible, then
F
invertible
\Leftrightarrow

(D-CA^{-1}B)
invertible. Moreover,

\displaystyle F^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}A^{-1}+A^{-1}B(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}CA^{-1}&\hskip 9.24994pt-A^{-1}B(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}\\
-(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}CA^{-1}&(D-CA^{-1}B)^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

(ii)

If
D
is invertible, then
F
invertible
\Leftrightarrow

(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}
invertible. Moreover,

\displaystyle F^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}&-(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}BD^{-1}\\
-D^{-1}C(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}&\hskip 9.24994ptD^{-1}+D^{-1}C(A-BD^{-1}C)^{-1}BD^{-1}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Proof.

This is a standard result
(e.g.,
Magnus and Neudecker 1999/1988
, Chap. 1 sec. 11)
.
∎

Corollary 2

(Inverse of a
2\times 2
partitioned matrix in a special case)
.

Let
E
be a square matrix s.t.

\displaystyle E=\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\prime}&0\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Then,

(i)

If
A
and
B^{\prime}A^{-1}B
are invertible, then
E
in invertible; and

(ii)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\prime}&0\\
\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}A^{-1}-A^{-1}B\left(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B\right)^{-1}B^{\prime}A^{-1}&A^{-1}B\left(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B\right)^{-1}\\
\left(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B\right)^{-1}B^{\prime}A^{-1}&-\left(B^{\prime}A^{-1}B\right)^{-1}\end{array}\right]
.

Proof.

Apply the above Lemma
33
i with
F=E
,
C=B^{\prime}
and
D=0
.
∎

Appendix E
More on the numerical example

The simulations were performed in R. Each model parameterization is simulated 10,000 times. The robustness of the simulation results was checked with different optimization algoritheorems, starting values and tolerance parameter values. The estimation for a single sample is typically performed in less than a few seconds. The calculations were done on a 24 CPU cores of a Dell server with 4 AMD Opteron 8425 HE processors running at 2.1 GHz. We numerically checked that the reported statistics have a converging behaviour as we increase the number of simulated samples to 10,000.

Appendix F
More on the empirical example

In empirical consumption-based asset pricing, the literature has found little common ground about the value of the relative risk aversion (RRA) of the representative agent: In most studies, point estimates from economically similar moment conditions are generally outside of each other’s confidence intervals. Section
4.2
(p.
4.2
) and the present appendix revisit the estimation of the RRA. The popularity of moment-based estimation in consumption-based asset pricing, and more generally in economics is due to the fact that moment-based estimation does not necessarily require the specification of a family of distributions for the data
(e.g.
Hansen 2013
, sec. 3)
. Typically, an economic model does not imply such family of distributions, except for tractability reasons. Imposing a family of distributions makes it difficult to disentangle the part of the inference results due to the empirical relevance of the economic model from the part due to these additional restrictions. Under regularity conditions, assuming a distribution corresponds to imposing an infinite number of extra moment restrictions
(e.g.,
Feller 1971 (1966
, 1971/1966, chap. VII, sec. 3)
.

In Section
4.2
(p.
4.2
) and the present appendix, we rely on the moment condition (
11
) on p.
11
.
This moment condition has several advantages. Firstly, it is as consistent with
Lucas 1978
as with more recent consumption-based asset-pricing models, such as
Barro 2006
or
Gabaix 2012
. In other words, despite its simplicity it also correspond to sophisticated models, and it allows us to obtain estimates that are robust to different variations of consumption-based asset pricing theory. Secondly, without loss of generality, it does not require to estimate the time discount rate, about which there is little debate: The time discount rate of the representative agent is consistently found to be between .9 and 1.
Note also that it has been common to use moment conditions with a separate parameter for
the so-called intertemporal elasticity of substitution, i.e., use Epstein-Zin-Weil preferences
(
Epstein and Zin 1991
, e.g.)
. However,
Bommier et al. 2017
show that such a specification makes the economic interpretation of the parameters difficult. In particular, they show that an increase of the so-called RRA (relative risk-aversion) parameter does not yield a behaviour that would be considered more risk averse. E.g., All other things being equal, savings can be a decreasing function of the so-called RRA parameter for an agent with Epstein-Zin-Weil preferences
(e.g.,
Bommier et al. 2017
, sec. 6)
. This difficulty of interpretation comes from a violation of the monotonicity axiom according to which an agent does not choose an action if another available action is preferable in every state of the world.

F.1.
Additional empirical evidence

Table 3.
ET vs. ESP inference (1890–2009)

Empirical moment condition:
\frac{1}{2009-1889}\sum_{t=1890}^{2009}\left[\left(\frac{C_{t}}{C_{t-1}}\right)^{-\theta}(R_{m,t}-R_{f,t})\right]=0
, where

R_{m,t}:=
gross market return,
R_{f,t}:=
risk-free asset gross return,
C_{t}:=
consumption,

and
\theta:=
relative risk aversion;

Normalized ET:=

;

Normalized ESP:=
